## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) â€” highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** â€” override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** â€” lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to youâ€”follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" [shape=box];
    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];
    "Has checklist?" [shape=diamond];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];
    "Follow skill exactly" [shape=box];
    "Respond (including clarifications)" [shape=doublecircle];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];
    "Invoke Skill tool" -> "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'";
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";
    "Has checklist?" -> "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [label="yes"];
    "Has checklist?" -> "Follow skill exactly" [label="no"];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" -> "Follow skill exactly";
}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOPâ€”you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept â‰  using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" â†’ brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" â†’ debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

# Brainstorming Ideas Into Designs

Help turn ideas into fully formed designs and specs through natural collaborative dialogue.

Start by understanding the current project context, then ask questions one at a time to refine the idea. Once you understand what you're building, present the design and get user approval.

<HARD-GATE>
Do NOT invoke any implementation skill, write any code, scaffold any project, or take any implementation action until you have presented a design and the user has approved it. This applies to EVERY project regardless of perceived simplicity.
</HARD-GATE>

## Anti-Pattern: "This Is Too Simple To Need A Design"

Every project goes through this process. A todo list, a single-function utility, a config change â€” all of them. "Simple" projects are where unexamined assumptions cause the most wasted work. The design can be short (a few sentences for truly simple projects), but you MUST present it and get approval.

## Checklist

You MUST create a task for each of these items and complete them in order:

1. **Explore project context** â€” check files, docs, recent commits
2. **Offer visual companion** (if topic will involve visual questions) â€” this is its own message, not combined with a clarifying question. See the Visual Companion section below.
3. **Ask clarifying questions** â€” one at a time, understand purpose/constraints/success criteria
4. **Propose 2-3 approaches** â€” with trade-offs and your recommendation
5. **Present design** â€” in sections scaled to their complexity, get user approval after each section
6. **Write design doc** â€” save to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md` and commit
7. **Spec self-review** â€” quick inline check for placeholders, contradictions, ambiguity, scope (see below)
8. **User reviews written spec** â€” ask user to review the spec file before proceeding
9. **Transition to implementation** â€” invoke writing-plans skill to create implementation plan

## Process Flow

```dot
digraph brainstorming {
    "Explore project context" [shape=box];
    "Visual questions ahead?" [shape=diamond];
    "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" [shape=box];
    "Ask clarifying questions" [shape=box];
    "Propose 2-3 approaches" [shape=box];
    "Present design sections" [shape=box];
    "User approves design?" [shape=diamond];
    "Write design doc" [shape=box];
    "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)" [shape=box];
    "User reviews spec?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke writing-plans skill" [shape=doublecircle];

    "Explore project context" -> "Visual questions ahead?";
    "Visual questions ahead?" -> "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" [label="yes"];
    "Visual questions ahead?" -> "Ask clarifying questions" [label="no"];
    "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" -> "Ask clarifying questions";
    "Ask clarifying questions" -> "Propose 2-3 approaches";
    "Propose 2-3 approaches" -> "Present design sections";
    "Present design sections" -> "User approves design?";
    "User approves design?" -> "Present design sections" [label="no, revise"];
    "User approves design?" -> "Write design doc" [label="yes"];
    "Write design doc" -> "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)";
    "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)" -> "User reviews spec?";
    "User reviews spec?" -> "Write design doc" [label="changes requested"];
    "User reviews spec?" -> "Invoke writing-plans skill" [label="approved"];
}
```

**The terminal state is invoking writing-plans.** Do NOT invoke frontend-design, mcp-builder, or any other implementation skill. The ONLY skill you invoke after brainstorming is writing-plans.

## The Process

**Understanding the idea:**

- Check out the current project state first (files, docs, recent commits)
- Before asking detailed questions, assess scope: if the request describes multiple independent subsystems (e.g., "build a platform with chat, file storage, billing, and analytics"), flag this immediately. Don't spend questions refining details of a project that needs to be decomposed first.
- If the project is too large for a single spec, help the user decompose into sub-projects: what are the independent pieces, how do they relate, what order should they be built? Then brainstorm the first sub-project through the normal design flow. Each sub-project gets its own spec â†’ plan â†’ implementation cycle.
- For appropriately-scoped projects, ask questions one at a time to refine the idea
- Prefer multiple choice questions when possible, but open-ended is fine too
- Only one question per message - if a topic needs more exploration, break it into multiple questions
- Focus on understanding: purpose, constraints, success criteria

**Exploring approaches:**

- Propose 2-3 different approaches with trade-offs
- Present options conversationally with your recommendation and reasoning
- Lead with your recommended option and explain why

**Presenting the design:**

- Once you believe you understand what you're building, present the design
- Scale each section to its complexity: a few sentences if straightforward, up to 200-300 words if nuanced
- Ask after each section whether it looks right so far
- Cover: architecture, components, data flow, error handling, testing
- Be ready to go back and clarify if something doesn't make sense

**Design for isolation and clarity:**

- Break the system into smaller units that each have one clear purpose, communicate through well-defined interfaces, and can be understood and tested independently
- For each unit, you should be able to answer: what does it do, how do you use it, and what does it depend on?
- Can someone understand what a unit does without reading its internals? Can you change the internals without breaking consumers? If not, the boundaries need work.
- Smaller, well-bounded units are also easier for you to work with - you reason better about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. When a file grows large, that's often a signal that it's doing too much.

**Working in existing codebases:**

- Explore the current structure before proposing changes. Follow existing patterns.
- Where existing code has problems that affect the work (e.g., a file that's grown too large, unclear boundaries, tangled responsibilities), include targeted improvements as part of the design - the way a good developer improves code they're working in.
- Don't propose unrelated refactoring. Stay focused on what serves the current goal.

## After the Design

**Documentation:**

- Write the validated design (spec) to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md`
  - (User preferences for spec location override this default)
- Use elements-of-style:writing-clearly-and-concisely skill if available
- Commit the design document to git

**Spec Self-Review:**
After writing the spec document, look at it with fresh eyes:

1. **Placeholder scan:** Any "TBD", "TODO", incomplete sections, or vague requirements? Fix them.
2. **Internal consistency:** Do any sections contradict each other? Does the architecture match the feature descriptions?
3. **Scope check:** Is this focused enough for a single implementation plan, or does it need decomposition?
4. **Ambiguity check:** Could any requirement be interpreted two different ways? If so, pick one and make it explicit.

Fix any issues inline. No need to re-review â€” just fix and move on.

**User Review Gate:**
After the spec review loop passes, ask the user to review the written spec before proceeding:

> "Spec written and committed to `<path>`. Please review it and let me know if you want to make any changes before we start writing out the implementation plan."

Wait for the user's response. If they request changes, make them and re-run the spec review loop. Only proceed once the user approves.

**Implementation:**

- Invoke the writing-plans skill to create a detailed implementation plan
- Do NOT invoke any other skill. writing-plans is the next step.

## Key Principles

- **One question at a time** - Don't overwhelm with multiple questions
- **Multiple choice preferred** - Easier to answer than open-ended when possible
- **YAGNI ruthlessly** - Remove unnecessary features from all designs
- **Explore alternatives** - Always propose 2-3 approaches before settling
- **Incremental validation** - Present design, get approval before moving on
- **Be flexible** - Go back and clarify when something doesn't make sense

## Visual Companion

A browser-based companion for showing mockups, diagrams, and visual options during brainstorming. Available as a tool â€” not a mode. Accepting the companion means it's available for questions that benefit from visual treatment; it does NOT mean every question goes through the browser.

**Offering the companion:** When you anticipate that upcoming questions will involve visual content (mockups, layouts, diagrams), offer it once for consent:
> "Some of what we're working on might be easier to explain if I can show it to you in a web browser. I can put together mockups, diagrams, comparisons, and other visuals as we go. This feature is still new and can be token-intensive. Want to try it? (Requires opening a local URL)"

**This offer MUST be its own message.** Do not combine it with clarifying questions, context summaries, or any other content. The message should contain ONLY the offer above and nothing else. Wait for the user's response before continuing. If they decline, proceed with text-only brainstorming.

**Per-question decision:** Even after the user accepts, decide FOR EACH QUESTION whether to use the browser or the terminal. The test: **would the user understand this better by seeing it than reading it?**

- **Use the browser** for content that IS visual â€” mockups, wireframes, layout comparisons, architecture diagrams, side-by-side visual designs
- **Use the terminal** for content that is text â€” requirements questions, conceptual choices, tradeoff lists, A/B/C/D text options, scope decisions

A question about a UI topic is not automatically a visual question. "What does personality mean in this context?" is a conceptual question â€” use the terminal. "Which wizard layout works better?" is a visual question â€” use the browser.

If they agree to the companion, read the detailed guide before proceeding:
`skills/brainstorming/visual-companion.md`

\title[Component Gerbes and Homological Quotients]{Component Gerbes and Homological Quotients over Teams and Presheaves}
\author{Haobo Ma}
\address[Haobo Ma]{AELF PTE LTD., \#14-02, Marina Bay Financial Centre Tower 1, 8 Marina Blvd, Singapore 018981}
\email[Haobo Ma]{auric@aelf.io}

\author{Wenlin Zhang}
\address[Wenlin Zhang]{National University of Singapore, Singapore}
\email[Wenlin Zhang]{e1327962@u.nus.edu}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
On the chosen good-cover site of a wedge of $\beta$ $2$-spheres, a nonzero finite abelian group $G$ occurs as a two-component label-loss kernel with complementary nonzero image subgroups exactly when $d(G)\leq 2\beta$ and $G$ is not a cyclic $p$-group.  This follows from the universal coefficient theorem after fixing a finite nerve, a constant finite abelian band, and a banded groupoid lift of a presheaf.  For a component-gerbe class $\omega$, evaluation on $H_2$ gives $\operatorname{ev}_\omega:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A$; its image and cokernel determine the componentwise and aggregate quotients.  When $H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0$ and $H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta$, the possible image subgroups are exactly those generated by at most $\beta$ elements.  Under explicit separatedness, representability, and \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison hypotheses, matching families without global amalgamations correspond to non-neutral component gerbes.  This derived $H^2$ invariant is not the relative \v{C}ech $H^1$ obstruction of Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa; only the underlying support-presheaf comparison is functorial, and the finite-group characterization does not transfer to bare empirical models.
\end{abstract}

\keywords{teams, presheaves, sheafification, banded gerbes, homological cokernel, universal coefficient theorem}
\subjclass[2020]{03B45, 03F55, 03G30, 18F20}

 succeeded in 160ms:
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Fix an integer $\beta\geq 1$.  Our main classification theorem concerns the
good-cover intersection site obtained from barycentric open-star covers of a
finite model of the wedge $\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2$.  A nonzero finite
abelian group $G$ occurs as the kernel of the canonical map
\[
 A/\bigl(K_1\cap K_2\bigr)\longrightarrow A/\bigl(K_1+K_2\bigr)
\]
for two non-neutral component gerbes with $K_1\cap K_2=0$ and
$K_1+K_2=G$ if and only if
\[
 d(G)\leq 2\beta
 \qquad\text{and}\qquad
 G\text{ is not a cyclic }p\text{-group}.
\]
Here $d(G)$ is the minimal number of generators of $G$.  In the terminology
fixed below, this kernel is the \emph{component-forgetting kernel}; the
displayed special case is a two-component label-loss kernel with complementary
nonzero image subgroups.  The statement is an existence and classification
result for the chosen site, band, and lift data, not an invariant of an
arbitrary empirical model.

The standard input is as follows.  We use universal satisfaction on nonempty
teams of assignments, presheaves and their sheafifications, stackification of
categories fibred in groupoids, the classification of banded gerbes by
Giraud's $H^2$, the \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison on the specified Leray
covers, the universal coefficient theorem, and elementary structure theory
and duality for finite abelian groups
\cite{Giraud1971,Breen2006,Hatcher2002}.  Our contributions are the organization
of these inputs into the stated semantic framework; the comparison between
matching families and component gerbes under explicit lift hypotheses; the
presentation-comparison groupoid and the two aggregate quotients; the
component-forgetting exact sequence; the construction of prescribed
componentwise homological images; and the preceding finite-group
classification.

The relation with the Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa obstruction must be stated
at the outset.  Their obstruction is a relative \v{C}ech class
\[
 \gamma_e(s)\in
 \check H^1(\mathcal M,\mathcal F_{e,\overline C_0})
\]
of a presheaf, equivalently a torsor class indexed by a chosen local section
\cite{AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012,Caru2017}.  Our obstruction is the
derived Giraud class
\[
 \omega_v=[\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C/a,\Band)
\]
of a gerbe banded by a sheaf, indexed by a global component section.  These
classes do not correspond.  What is functorial is only the support-level
comparison: every finite Abramsky--Brandenburger empirical model gives a
canonical split stack whose component gerbes are neutral.  In particular, a
strongly contextual model has no global component section from which our map
could produce its local-section-indexed AMB classes.  Consequently the
condition $d(G)\leq2\beta$ with $G$ not a cyclic $p$-group does not transfer to
bare empirical models; a band and a non-split banded stack lift would have to
be supplied as additional data.  This is proved precisely in
\Cref{thm:empirical-model-stack-interface,thm:unique-branch-contextuality-comparison}.

We now describe the framework.  An information state
$p=(\Gamma_p,R_p)$ is a nonempty team $R_p$ of assignments with common finite
context $\Gamma_p$.  We write $M,p\Vdash\varphi$ when every assignment in
$R_p$ satisfies $\varphi$ in the ordinary Tarskian sense.  Thus $\Vdash$ is
universal team satisfaction, not set-theoretic, Kripke, Beth, or
Kripke--Joyal forcing.  Refinement enlarges the context and may restrict the
team, which immediately gives persistence.  Point assignments enter through
the singleton-state embedding.  The later semantic frameworks add typed data
while preserving the truth of formulas in the reduct; we call these
truth-preserving reduct expansions because consequence conservativity is not asserted without an expansion
theorem for every lower model.

For an admissible parameter $r$ at $p$, the paper specifies a thin
reference-refinement site $\mathcal C_{p,s}$, its base object
$a=[r]_p$, and a presheaf of local sections
$F_{p,s}:\mathcal C_{p,s}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}$.  A matching family
without an element of $F_{p,s}(a)$ is the set-valued failure studied here.
The forward implication from a matching family to a section of the
sheafification is formal; the converse holds when
$F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_{p,s}/a}$ is separated
(\Cref{prop:compsec-to-sheafification,thm:sheafification-characterization}).
The predicates expressing a matching family, a global section, and matching
without amalgamation are not definable in the automorphism-invariant,
constant-free, one-variable fragment of the lower reduct
(\Cref{thm:pointwise-irreducibility}).

Gerbe-valued obstructions require extra, noncanonical structure.  A
gluing-sensitive banded lift datum consists of a banded groupoid lift of $F$,
its stackification together with an identification of its component sheaf
with $aF$, essential surjectivity on the terminal fibre, and a
\v{C}ech-to-derived comparison hypothesis.  A section
$v\in\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a)\cong(aF)(a)$ determines a full component gerbe
$\mathfrak G_v$ and hence a Giraud class $\omega_v$.  Under the stated
separatedness, representability, and comparison hypotheses, matching without
amalgamation is equivalent to the existence of global component sections with
all corresponding component gerbes non-neutral
(\Cref{thm:component-gerbe-decomposition,thm:gerbe-null-semantics}).  A
strictly pullback-stable representative choice imposes a useful rigidity
condition: together with terminal-fibre essential surjectivity it forces the
sheafification unit to be onto at the terminal object
(\Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}).

Fix next a global component section, a finite nerve representative, and a
constant finite abelian band $A$.  The universal coefficient map evaluates
the class on second homology,
\[
 \operatorname{ev}_{\omega}:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A.
\]
We write $K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)$ for its
homological image and $Q_\omega=\operatorname{coker}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)
=A/K_\omega$ for its homological cokernel.  These are invariants of an object
of the presentation-comparison groupoid and transport functorially along the
chosen comparison zigzags; no path independence is claimed.  A class with
zero $H_2$ evaluation is precisely a class in the $\operatorname{Ext}^1$
kernel of the universal coefficient sequence.  This separates the
homology-evaluation quotient from the more presentation-dependent quotient by
the subgroup generated by the coordinates of a chosen cocycle.

For finitely many component sections $v$, put $K_v=\operatorname{Im}
(\operatorname{ev}_{\omega_v})$.  Retaining the component label gives the
label-aware quotient $A/(\bigcap_vK_v)$; retaining only characters that work
for every component gives the common-character quotient
$A/(\sum_vK_v)$.  Their canonical comparison has kernel
\[
 \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}.
\]
The exact sequence and its finite-register interpretation are formal.  The
existence results are not: on the finite intersection site associated to a
connected good-cover nerve with $H_1=0$, we construct typed expansions whose
componentwise evaluation maps have prescribed images, including mixtures of
neutral and non-neutral component gerbes.  If $H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong
\mathbb Z^\beta$, the attainable image subgroups are exactly those generated
by at most $\beta$ elements.  The main classification follows by asking when
$G$ decomposes as $K_1\oplus K_2$ with both summands nonzero and each generated
by at most $\beta$ elements.

The paper is organized as follows.  Sections~\ref{sec:preliminaries} and
\ref{sec:information-states} define the semantic frameworks and universal
team satisfaction.  Section~\ref{sec:null-decomposition} treats presheaves,
sheafification, and lower-fragment non-definability.  The component-gerbe and
Giraud-class results follow, then the finite-presentation and homological
quotients, their aggregation over component sections, the construction
theorems, the wedge-of-spheres classification, and the precise empirical-model
comparison.  The appendix records supplementary results on indexed
refinements and the GHZ/Mermin example.

\subsection*{Glossary of retained terminology}

The following short names are used only after their standard-language content
has been fixed.

\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=2em,labelindent=0pt]
\item[Information state $p=(\Gamma,R)$.]
A nonempty team $R$ of assignments with common finite context $\Gamma$.
\item[Universal team satisfaction.]
$M,p\Vdash\varphi$ means that every assignment in the team of $p$ satisfies
$\varphi$ in ordinary Tarskian semantics.
\item[Singleton-state embedding.]
The map from an assignment to its singleton team, preserving ordinary
satisfaction.
\item[Truth-preserving reduct expansion.]
An expansion whose higher models preserve lower-language satisfaction after
the specified model reduct and state projection; consequence conservativity
is not part of the definition.
\item[Typed expansion class.]
The class of all supplied higher-layer data of the stipulated types over a
fixed lower model.
\item[Admissible parameter $r$.]
An element satisfying the primitive predicate $\Adm_s$; the predicate records
exactly that the site and presheaf data stipulated in the relevant typed model
are attached to $r$.
\item[{Base object $a=[r]_p$.}]
The object of the reference-refinement site associated to the pair $(p,r)$;
the brackets do not denote an equivalence class.
\item[Reference-refinement site $\mathcal C_{p,s}$.]
The thin category of base objects ordered by refinement, equipped with the
specified pretopology and pullbacks.
\item[Presheaf of local sections $F_{p,s}$.]
The functor $\mathcal C_{p,s}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}$.
\item[Matching without amalgamation at $a$.]
A matching family exists on a cover of $a$, but $F_{p,s}(a)$ is empty.
\item[Gluing-sensitive banded lift datum.]
A banded groupoid lift, its stackification and component-sheaf identification,
terminal-fibre essential surjectivity, and a \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison
hypothesis.
\item[Pullback-stable representative choice.]
A choice of one representative of each presheaf section that is preserved
strictly by every pullback; ``representative-rigid'' abbreviates this condition.
\item[Global component section $v$.]
An element of $\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a)$, identified with a section of
$(aF)(a)$.
\item[Component gerbe $\mathfrak G_v$ and component obstruction $\omega_v$.]
The full banded substack over $v$ and its Giraud class
$[\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C/a,\Band)$.
\item[Presentation-comparison groupoid.]
The groupoid freely generated by the specified refinements, cocycle
replacements, band retrivializations, and their formal inverses; distinct
zigzags need not give the same comparison.
\item[Coordinate-image subgroup and cocycle-coordinate quotient.]
For a chosen cocycle $\alpha$, the subgroup generated by its coordinate values
and the corresponding quotient $A/H_\alpha$.
\item[Homological image $K_\omega$ and homological cokernel $Q_\omega$.]
$\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)$ and
$\operatorname{coker}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)=A/K_\omega$.
\item[Label-aware quotient.]
$A/(\bigcap_vK_v)$, equivalently the image of $A$ in the product of the
componentwise homological cokernels.
\item[Common-character quotient.]
$A/(\sum_vK_v)$, detected by the characters that annihilate every $K_v$.
\item[Component-forgetting kernel.]
The kernel of the canonical map from the label-aware quotient to the
common-character quotient, namely $(\sum_vK_v)/(\bigcap_vK_v)$.
\item[Fibre-separating auxiliary register.]
A finite set used in an injective lift of a quotient map; its minimum
cardinality is the largest quotient-fibre cardinality.
\item[Finite intersection site associated to a cover nerve.]
The finite thin pullback site generated by the formal intersections indexed by
the nerve, with an initial object representing empty intersections.
\item[Typed readout.]
The partial output attached to an admissible parameter: inadmissible if no
typed data are attached, uniquely determined if the sheafified global section
is unique, and non-unique-or-absent otherwise.
\item[Indexed refinement system.]
A family of state-refinement relations indexed by information coordinates,
each monotone for the state preorder and the specified time order.
\item[Information-determining axis set.]
A finite set of refinement coordinates whose information values determine any
decided truth value among the states reachable along those coordinates.
\item[Pairwise commuting refinements and common refinement.]
Refinements for which every indicated finite family has a state refining all
its members.
\item[Readout-preserving rewrite.]
A rewrite between partial outputs that preserves both definedness and value.
\item[Banded groupoid lift of a support presheaf.]
A category fibred in groupoids whose presheaf of isomorphism classes is the
given empirical-model support presheaf and whose automorphism sheaves are the
specified band.
\end{description}


 succeeded in 167ms:
\section{Concluding remarks}\label{sec:conclusion}

The paper separates three stages of the local-to-global problem. At the level
of information states, \Cref{thm:pointwise-irreducibility} shows that neither
existence of a matching family nor matching without amalgamation is definable
in the automorphism-invariant, constant-free, one-variable fragment considered
here. This is not a general expressivity theorem; it identifies the first
point at which the presheaf data exceed the lower language of universal team
satisfaction.

The passage from set-valued to groupoid-valued data necessarily depends on
additional lift data. By \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}, a strictly
pullback-stable representative choice together with essential surjectivity on
the terminal fibre implies that the sheafification unit is surjective there.
Under $H^1$-vanishing and separatedness of the restriction to the slice site,
this is equivalent to the presheaf already satisfying the sheaf condition at
that fibre. For finite empirical models,
\Cref{thm:empirical-model-stack-interface} gives a canonical split banded
stack lift, but every component gerbe is neutral. The AMB obstruction is a
relative \v{C}ech $H^1$ torsor class indexed by a local section, whereas the
obstruction here is a derived Giraud $H^2$ class indexed by a global component
section. Only the support-level comparison is functorial, and neither class
recovers the other in general.

For a fixed finite nerve representative, the homological image
$K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)$ and homological
cokernel $Q_\omega=A/K_\omega$ record the contribution detected on $H_2$.
Across presentations they transport along chosen arrows in the
presentation-comparison groupoid; no independence of the chosen zigzag is
asserted. For several global component sections, the label-aware and
common-character quotients are related by the component-forgetting exact
sequence. Under the fixed finite-nerve, constant-band,
\v{C}ech-to-derived comparison, and $H_1=0$ hypotheses, the construction
theorems attain exactly the componentwise image subgroups permitted by
second homology.

When $H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta$, these are precisely
the subgroups generated by at most $\beta$ elements. The chosen open-star good
covers of a wedge of $\beta$ $2$-spheres therefore give the exact
classification: the nonzero finite abelian groups occurring as two-component
label-loss kernels with complementary nonzero image subgroups are those with
$d(G)\leq2\beta$ that are not cyclic $p$-groups. This construction does not
transfer to bare empirical models, whose canonical split lift has zero
homological image subgroups.

The hypotheses remain explicit throughout: \textup{(RC)} specifies pullbacks
of the presheaf data; \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H4)} specify the banded lift,
representability, and \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison; separatedness is invoked
where global component sections are represented by matching families; and
$H^1$-vanishing is used for terminal-fibre rigidity. The formulation here uses
pullback-based slice sites. Comparisons of finite presentations are therefore
groupoid-indexed and zigzag-dependent, rather than presentation-free choices
of canonical representatives.

\section{Concluding remarks}\label{sec:conclusion}

The paper separates three stages of the local-to-global problem. At the level
of information states, \Cref{thm:pointwise-irreducibility} shows that neither
existence of a matching family nor matching without amalgamation is definable
in the automorphism-invariant, constant-free, one-variable fragment considered
here. This is not a general expressivity theorem; it identifies the first
point at which the presheaf data exceed the lower language of universal team
satisfaction.

The passage from set-valued to groupoid-valued data necessarily depends on
additional lift data. By \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}, a strictly
pullback-stable representative choice together with essential surjectivity on
the terminal fibre implies that the sheafification unit is surjective there.
Under $H^1$-vanishing and separatedness of the restriction to the slice site,
this is equivalent to the presheaf already satisfying the sheaf condition at
that fibre. For finite empirical models,
\Cref{thm:empirical-model-stack-interface} gives a canonical split banded
stack lift, but every component gerbe is neutral. The AMB obstruction is a
relative \v{C}ech $H^1$ torsor class indexed by a local section, whereas the
obstruction here is a derived Giraud $H^2$ class indexed by a global component
section. Only the support-level comparison is functorial, and neither class
recovers the other in general.

For a fixed finite nerve representative, the homological image
$K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)$ and homological
cokernel $Q_\omega=A/K_\omega$ record the contribution detected on $H_2$.
Across presentations they transport along chosen arrows in the
presentation-comparison groupoid; no independence of the chosen zigzag is
asserted. For several global component sections, the label-aware and
common-character quotients are related by the component-forgetting exact
sequence. Under the fixed finite-nerve, constant-band,
\v{C}ech-to-derived comparison, and $H_1=0$ hypotheses, the construction
theorems attain exactly the componentwise image subgroups permitted by
second homology.

When $H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta$, these are precisely
the subgroups generated by at most $\beta$ elements. The chosen open-star good
covers of a wedge of $\beta$ $2$-spheres therefore give the exact
classification: the nonzero finite abelian groups occurring as two-component
label-loss kernels with complementary nonzero image subgroups are those with
$d(G)\leq2\beta$ that are not cyclic $p$-groups. This construction does not
transfer to bare empirical models, whose canonical split lift has zero
homological image subgroups.

The hypotheses remain explicit throughout: \textup{(RC)} specifies pullbacks
of the presheaf data; \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H4)} specify the banded lift,
representability, and \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison; separatedness is invoked
where global component sections are represented by matching families; and
$H^1$-vanishing is used for terminal-fibre rigidity. The formulation here uses
pullback-based slice sites. Comparisons of finite presentations are therefore
groupoid-indexed and zigzag-dependent, rather than presentation-free choices
of canonical representatives.


 succeeded in 157ms:
Target journal: APAL (Annals of Pure and Applied Logic)
Previously: NDJFL (changed after internal review)

Revision summary (2026-03-23):

1. Fixed correctness issue in Theorem 4.26 (component gerbe decomposition):
   The original version claimed the full stack decomposition unconditionally.
   The proof only showed each visible branch gives a gerbe, not that every
   local branch comes from a global one. Fix: the gerbe property is now the
   theorem; the full decomposition is a corollary under an explicit branch
   constancy hypothesis.

2. Fixed correctness issue in Theorem 4.28 (gerbe-null-semantics):
   The equivalence Sec_s(r) <=> L_r(a) nonempty used the direction from
   L_r(a) to Sec_s(r) without justification. Fix: added Definition
   (global conservativity at the terminal fibre) requiring essential
   surjectivity of the stackification morphism on the terminal fibre,
   and Proposition (no new global objects at a) deriving the equivalence.

3. Added forcing necessity theorem (pointwise irreducibility of gluing
   predicates): Two admitted references are constructed that are
   indistinguishable at L_1 but one has CompSec /\ Sec while the other
   has CompSec /\ not Sec. This proves that the local-object enrichment
   is not merely a convenience but introduces genuinely new predicates.

4. Corrected the connection to sheaf-theoretic contextuality: finite support
   presheaves have canonical refinement-functorial split stacks, but their
   Giraud classes vanish. The AMB obstruction is a relative H1 torsor class
   indexed by a local section, so it is not recovered or refined by the
   manuscript's H2 branch map without additional degree-shifting data.

5. Removed all references to "larger architecture", "broader research
   program", "eleven-layer architecture". The paper is now presented as
   a self-contained four-layer fragment.

6. Moved finite-state complexity upper bounds from main text to appendix.

7. Split sec_null_decomposition.tex (1689 lines) into three files under
   800 lines each.

8. Updated abstract, introduction, preliminaries, and conclusion to
   reflect all changes.

Residual limitations:
1. The paper gives upper bounds for the support problems but not matching
   lower bounds. This is now stated as an open problem.
2. The realization layer remains abstract.


 succeeded in 157ms:
# Theorem Inventory

This Stage A inventory was prepared against `scope_contract.md`, `scope_contract.json`, `research_directive.md`, the compiled manuscript sources included by `main.tex`, all `.tex` files in the paper directory, and `references.bib`.

## Summary

The compiled manuscript contains the A/B/C theorem chain: extension-chain forcing, pullback-based local sites and sheafification, terminal-fibre rigidity, lower-fragment separation, component gerbes and gerbe-null semantics, strict and intrinsic visible quotients, pure Ext character blindness, branch-sensitive versus branch-uniform comparison, finite-nerve realization, the torsion-free H2 subgroup classification, bouquet two-branch resolution, the exact obstruction to transferring these results to empirical models, and the split GHZ/Mermin lift.

No required in-scope theorem from the scope contract is completely absent from the compiled manuscript. The main risks are not missing labels but proof-interface sharpness and source hygiene:

- `lem:bouquet-cover-classical-interface` uses `beta` without quantifying it.
- `lem:generated-subsite-model-interface` needs an explicit admissible-model/free-expansion convention.
- `prop:invariance-visible-quotients` must remain groupoid-indexed, not path-independent.
- `sec_forcing_necessity.tex` is an unused stronger draft with duplicate `thm:pointwise-irreducibility` and unresolved references.
- `sequel/` files are out-of-scope split material.
- Several included section files exceed the 800-line discipline.

## Files Written

- `theorem_inventory.json`
- `theorem_inventory.md`

## JSON Inventory

The authoritative machine-readable inventory is in `theorem_inventory.json`. It uses exactly the required top-level keys:

- `valid`
- `in_scope_present`

 succeeded in 146ms:
\section{Concluding remarks}\label{sec:conclusion}

The paper separates three stages of the local-to-global problem. At the level
of information states, \Cref{thm:pointwise-irreducibility} shows that neither
existence of a matching family nor matching without amalgamation is definable
in the automorphism-invariant, constant-free, one-variable fragment considered
here. This is not a general expressivity theorem; it identifies the first
point at which the presheaf data exceed the lower language of universal team
satisfaction.

The passage from set-valued to groupoid-valued data necessarily depends on
additional lift data. By \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}, a strictly
pullback-stable representative choice together with essential surjectivity on
the terminal fibre implies that the sheafification unit is surjective there.
Under $H^1$-vanishing and separatedness of the restriction to the slice site,
this is equivalent to the presheaf already satisfying the sheaf condition at
that fibre. For finite empirical models,
\Cref{thm:empirical-model-stack-interface} gives a canonical split banded
stack lift, but every component gerbe is neutral. The AMB obstruction is a
relative \v{C}ech $H^1$ torsor class indexed by a local section, whereas the
obstruction here is a derived Giraud $H^2$ class indexed by a global component
section. Only the support-level comparison is functorial, and neither class
recovers the other in general.

For a fixed finite nerve representative, the homological image
$K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)$ and homological
cokernel $Q_\omega=A/K_\omega$ record the contribution detected on $H_2$.
Across presentations they transport along chosen arrows in the
presentation-comparison groupoid; no independence of the chosen zigzag is
asserted. For several global component sections, the label-aware and
common-character quotients are related by the component-forgetting exact
sequence. Under the fixed finite-nerve, constant-band,
\v{C}ech-to-derived comparison, and $H_1=0$ hypotheses, the construction
theorems attain exactly the componentwise image subgroups permitted by
second homology.

When $H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta$, these are precisely
the subgroups generated by at most $\beta$ elements. The chosen open-star good
covers of a wedge of $\beta$ $2$-spheres therefore give the exact
classification: the nonzero finite abelian groups occurring as two-component
label-loss kernels with complementary nonzero image subgroups are those with
$d(G)\leq2\beta$ that are not cyclic $p$-groups. This construction does not
transfer to bare empirical models, whose canonical split lift has zero
homological image subgroups.

The hypotheses remain explicit throughout: \textup{(RC)} specifies pullbacks
of the presheaf data; \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H4)} specify the banded lift,
representability, and \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison; separatedness is invoked
where global component sections are represented by matching families; and
$H^1$-vanishing is used for terminal-fibre rigidity. The formulation here uses
pullback-based slice sites. Comparisons of finite presentations are therefore
groupoid-indexed and zigzag-dependent, rather than presentation-free choices
of canonical representatives.


 succeeded in 159ms:
Target journal: APAL (Annals of Pure and Applied Logic)
Previously: NDJFL (changed after internal review)

Revision summary (2026-03-23):

1. Fixed correctness issue in Theorem 4.26 (component gerbe decomposition):
   The original version claimed the full stack decomposition unconditionally.
   The proof only showed each visible branch gives a gerbe, not that every
   local branch comes from a global one. Fix: the gerbe property is now the
   theorem; the full decomposition is a corollary under an explicit branch
   constancy hypothesis.

2. Fixed correctness issue in Theorem 4.28 (gerbe-null-semantics):
   The equivalence Sec_s(r) <=> L_r(a) nonempty used the direction from
   L_r(a) to Sec_s(r) without justification. Fix: added Definition
   (global conservativity at the terminal fibre) requiring essential
   surjectivity of the stackification morphism on the terminal fibre,
   and Proposition (no new global objects at a) deriving the equivalence.

3. Added forcing necessity theorem (pointwise irreducibility of gluing
   predicates): Two admitted references are constructed that are
   indistinguishable at L_1 but one has CompSec /\ Sec while the other
   has CompSec /\ not Sec. This proves that the local-object enrichment
   is not merely a convenience but introduces genuinely new predicates.

4. Corrected the connection to sheaf-theoretic contextuality: finite support
   presheaves have canonical refinement-functorial split stacks, but their
   Giraud classes vanish. The AMB obstruction is a relative H1 torsor class
   indexed by a local section, so it is not recovered or refined by the
   manuscript's H2 branch map without additional degree-shifting data.

5. Removed all references to "larger architecture", "broader research
   program", "eleven-layer architecture". The paper is now presented as
   a self-contained four-layer fragment.

6. Moved finite-state complexity upper bounds from main text to appendix.

7. Split sec_null_decomposition.tex (1689 lines) into three files under
   800 lines each.

8. Updated abstract, introduction, preliminaries, and conclusion to
   reflect all changes.

Residual limitations:
1. The paper gives upper bounds for the support problems but not matching
   lower bounds. This is now stated as an open problem.
2. The realization layer remains abstract.

# Theorem Inventory

This Stage A inventory was prepared against `scope_contract.md`, `scope_contract.json`, `research_directive.md`, the compiled manuscript sources included by `main.tex`, all `.tex` files in the paper directory, and `references.bib`.

## Summary

The compiled manuscript contains the A/B/C theorem chain: extension-chain forcing, pullback-based local sites and sheafification, terminal-fibre rigidity, lower-fragment separation, component gerbes and gerbe-null semantics, strict and intrinsic visible quotients, pure Ext character blindness, branch-sensitive versus branch-uniform comparison, finite-nerve realization, the torsion-free H2 subgroup classification, bouquet two-branch resolution, the exact obstruction to transferring these results to empirical models, and the split GHZ/Mermin lift.

No required in-scope theorem from the scope contract is completely absent from the compiled manuscript. The main risks are not missing labels but proof-interface sharpness and source hygiene:

- `lem:bouquet-cover-classical-interface` uses `beta` without quantifying it.
- `lem:generated-subsite-model-interface` needs an explicit admissible-model/free-expansion convention.
- `prop:invariance-visible-quotients` must remain groupoid-indexed, not path-independent.
- `sec_forcing_necessity.tex` is an unused stronger draft with duplicate `thm:pointwise-irreducibility` and unresolved references.
- `sequel/` files are out-of-scope split material.
- Several included section files exceed the 800-line discipline.

.\sec_null_decomposition_part2.tex:534:\begin{remark}[Why tuples and parameters are excluded]\label{rem:tuple-parameter-limitation}
.\sec_null_decomposition_part2.tex:535:The restriction to one free variable and no parameters is essential for the argument. If two free reference variables are allowed, equality formulas such as $x_1=x_2$ already distinguish tuples by their equality pattern, so a single orbit no longer witnesses the undefinability of $\CompSec_s$ or $\Sec_s$. Likewise, if parameters of sort $\mathsf{Ref}_s$ are available, a formula of the form $x=b$ isolates a chosen witness unless one first arranges that all witnesses lie outside the parameter support. Thus the comparison class $\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\mathrm{inv}}$ records exactly the fragment in which automorphism-invariant pointwise semantics cannot detect existence of a matching family.

.\README.md:26:- 精确界定 empirical-model 接口：每个有限 support presheaf 都函子性地产生 split realization stack，但其 Giraud 类全为零；AMB 障碍是由局部截面索引的相对 $H^1$ torsor 类，不是本文由全局 component 索引的 $H^2$ gerbe 类。因而 bouquet 的 $d(G)\le 2\beta$ 分类不自动转移到 empirical models。

\subsection{The exact interface with empirical models}

Abramsky--Brandenburger empirical models already have two categorical
reformulations close to the question considered here. Car\`u identifies the
degree-one cohomology used by Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa with torsors
trivialized by the measurement cover \cite[Prop.~6.3]{Caru2017}. More recently,
Barbosa, Kharoof, and Okay construct fully faithful functors from measurement
scenarios to bundle and simplicial-bundle scenarios, together with natural
isomorphisms on their empirical-model functors
\cite{BarbosaKharoofOkay2024}. Neither construction produces a banded gerbe
or a banded stack lift in the sense of
\Cref{def:banded-stack-lift}. The following comparison locates the
gap precisely.

Let a \emph{finite measurement scenario} be a finite set \(X\), finite
nonempty outcome sets \(O_x\) for \(x\in X\), and a finite antichain
\(\mathcal M\) of subsets of \(X\) whose union is \(X\). Write
\(\mathcal D_{\mathcal M}\) for the finite context category consisting of
the subcontexts of members of \(\mathcal M\), with inclusions as arrows, and
equip it with the coverage induced by the maximal contexts. We also use its
terminal augmentation, obtained by adjoining \(X\) and declaring
\(\mathcal M\) to cover \(X\). A finite empirical model
\[
e=(e_C)_{C\in\mathcal M}
\]
is a compatible family of probability distributions on
\(\prod_{x\in C}O_x\). Its support presheaf is
\[
S_e(U)=\operatorname{supp}(e_U)
\qquad(U\in\mathcal D_{\mathcal M}),
\]
where compatibility makes the marginal \(e_U\) independent of the maximal
context containing \(U\) \cite{AbramskyBrandenburger2011}. At the adjoined
terminal object we take the right Kan extension
\[
\widehat S_e(X):=
\varprojlim_{U\in\mathcal D_{\mathcal M}}S_e(U).
\]
Thus an element of \(\widehat S_e(X)\) is, by definition, a compatible family
of supported local assignments. Below \(aS_e\) denotes the sheafification of
this terminally augmented presheaf.

A \emph{context refinement} used below is a refinement
\(\rho:\mathcal M'\to\mathcal M\) on the same measurements and outcomes,
with \(C'\subseteq\rho(C')\), for which marginalization defines the refined
model \(\rho^*e\). The inclusion of downward-closed context categories induces
a functor
\[
u_\rho:\mathcal D_{\mathcal M'}\hookrightarrow
\mathcal D_{\mathcal M}
\]
which is required to be continuous for the two generated cover topologies and
to extend to their terminal augmentations. We also require the natural
base-change isomorphism
\[
S_{\rho^*e}\cong u_\rho^*S_e.
\]
This includes ordinary iterated refinements which are covering refinements
for the generated topology. No
claim is made here for resource-theoretic simulations which change the
measurement or outcome sets.

\begin{proposition}[Abramsky--Brandenburger support interface]\label{prop:ab-compatibility-translation}
For every finite empirical model \(e\), the following sets are naturally in
bijection:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item compatible families \((s_C)_{C\in\mathcal M}\) with
      \(s_C\in S_e(C)\);
\item global assignments \(s\in\prod_{x\in X}O_x\) such that
      \(s|_C\in S_e(C)\) for every \(C\in\mathcal M\);
\item global sections of the support presheaf in the standard
      Abramsky--Brandenburger sense;
\item elements of \(\widehat S_e(X)\), equivalently elements of
      \((aS_e)(X)\) on the terminally augmented context site.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, if both \(\CompSec_s(r)\) and \(\Sec_s(r)\) are interpreted
from this same support presheaf on the standard measurement cover, then
\[
M,p\Vdash\CompSec_s(r)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
M,p\Vdash\Sec_s(r).
\]
In particular, strong contextuality is absence of a compatible family at the
set-valued level; it is not the matching-without-amalgamation case
\(\CompSec_s(r)\land\neg\Sec_s(r)\) of this paper.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The event presheaf
\(\mathcal E(U)=\prod_{x\in U}O_x\) is a sheaf. Hence a compatible family
\((s_C)\) glues uniquely to a global assignment \(s\), and its restrictions
remain in the prescribed supports. Conversely, restriction of such an
assignment gives a compatible supported family. This proves the first three
identifications. The fourth is the defining limit formula for
\(\widehat S_e(X)\); the plus construction for the cover
\(\mathcal M\to X\) gives the same set in the sheafification. The displayed
equivalence follows immediately.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Support versus probabilistic contextuality]\label{rem:support-vs-probabilistic-contextuality}
The proposition concerns the possibilistic support of \(e\). Probabilistic
noncontextuality asks instead for a distribution on
\(\prod_{x\in X}O_x\) whose marginals are the given distributions \(e_C\).
That is a fibre-extension problem for the distribution presheaf, and is not
determined by \(S_e\): empirical models with the same support can have
different probabilistic noncontextuality behaviour.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Canonical split stack and the degree obstruction]\label{thm:empirical-model-stack-interface}
Let \(e\) be a finite empirical model on the context site above.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item There is a canonical banded stack lift with zero band,
\[
\mathfrak E_e^0:=\operatorname{Disc}(aS_e),
\qquad
\pi_0(\mathfrak E_e^0)\cong aS_e.
\]
Here \(\operatorname{Disc}\) regards a sheaf of sets as a stack of discrete
groupoids.
\item More generally, after an abelian sheaf \(\Band\) has been supplied,
there is a canonical split \(\Band\)-banded stack lift
\[
\mathfrak E_e^{\mathrm{spl}}:=
\operatorname{Disc}(aS_e)\times\mathsf B\Band.
\]
Every component gerbe of this lift is neutral, and therefore every
Giraud class supplied by
\Cref{thm:intrinsic-branch-obstruction-extension} is zero.
\item The assignments in \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)} are pseudonatural
under context refinements: there are coherent banded equivalences
\[
u_\rho^{-1}\mathfrak E_e^0\simeq
\mathfrak E_{\rho^*e}^0,
\qquad
u_\rho^{-1}\mathfrak E_e^{\mathrm{spl}}\simeq
\mathfrak E_{\rho^*e}^{\mathrm{spl}}
\]
in the second formula whenever the supplied bands are identified under
pullback.
\item The AB and AMB constructions do not produce the non-split degree-two
data required by this paper. More precisely, fix
\(C_0\in\mathcal M\) and \(s\in S_e(C_0)\).
Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa form the free-abelian presheaf
\(\mathcal F_e=\mathbb Z[S_e]\), its relative kernel
\(\mathcal F_{e,\overline C_0}\), and a class
\[
\gamma_e(s)\in
\check H^1(\mathcal M,\mathcal F_{e,\overline C_0}).
\]
This is relative \v{C}ech cohomology of a presheaf, and the class is the
isomorphism class of an
\(\mathcal F_{e,\overline C_0}\)-torsor trivialized by
\(\mathcal M\). It is not a derived \(H^2\) Giraud class for a sheaf band
\cite{AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012,Caru2017}.
\item There is no comparison which recovers these AMB classes, for all finite
empirical models, from the component-section obstruction map of any banded stack lift
whose component sheaf is \(aS_e\). Indeed, if \(e\) is strongly contextual,
then
\[
(aS_e)(X)=\varnothing,
\]
so the component-section obstruction map
\[
\delta_{\mathfrak E_e}:(aS_e)(X)\longrightarrow
H^2(\mathcal D_{\mathcal M},\Band)
\]
has empty domain, whereas AMB classes are defined for the local sections
\(s\in S_e(C_0)\) and can be nonzero, as in their GHZ and
Kochen--Specker examples.
\end{enumerate}
In \textup{(i)}, \emph{canonical} means that the construction is the
composite of the universal sheafification functor and the discrete-stack
functor, with no choices, and that the displayed refinement equivalences
satisfy the usual pseudofunctor coherence. It does not mean that all
banded stack lifts of \(S_e\) are equivalent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The discrete groupoid construction preserves descent, so
\(\operatorname{Disc}(aS_e)\) is a stack and its connected-component sheaf is
exactly \(aS_e\). Its automorphism sheaves are zero, proving \textup{(i)}.
The classifying stack \(\mathsf B\Band\) has one local component and
automorphism sheaf \(\Band\). Taking its product with the discrete stack
therefore proves the band and component assertions in \textup{(ii)}. The
global object represented by the identity \(\Band\)-torsor neutralizes each
component gerbe, so its Giraud class vanishes.

Inverse image for the morphism of context sites commutes, up to its canonical
isomorphism, with associated sheaves, discrete stacks, products, and
classifying stacks. Together with
\(S_{\rho^*e}\cong u_\rho^*S_e\), this gives the equivalences and their
coherence in \textup{(iii)}.

The construction of \(\gamma_e(s)\) and its degree are those of
\cite[Sec.~4]{AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012}. The torsor
classification is \cite[Prop.~6.3]{Caru2017}, proving \textup{(iv)}. Finally,
\Cref{prop:ab-compatibility-translation} identifies strong contextuality with
emptiness of \((aS_e)(X)\). The component-section obstruction map of
\Cref{thm:intrinsic-branch-obstruction-extension} is indexed by precisely
that set, while \(\gamma_e(s)\) is indexed by a local section. This proves
the asserted failure in \textup{(v)}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exact limit of transfer to empirical models]\label{thm:unique-branch-contextuality-comparison}
For finite Abramsky--Brandenburger empirical models, the set-valued
local-to-global statements of \Cref{prop:ab-compatibility-translation}
transfer functorially. The later banded and homological theorems transfer
only after a band and a non-split banded stack lift have been
supplied as additional data. In particular:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item the canonical split lift gives only zero Giraud classes and zero
homological image subgroups;
\item the characterization
\[
d(G)\le 2\beta
\quad\text{and}\quad
G\text{ is not a cyclic }p\text{-group}
\]
remains a construction theorem for the chosen wedge-of-spheres good-cover, band, and
two-component cocycle data of
\Cref{thm:wedge-sphere-two-component-label-loss}; it is not a characterization of
groups canonically attached to empirical models;
\item without an additional degree-shifting construction, the Giraud
\(H^2\)-class neither recovers nor refines the AMB relative
\(\check H^1\)-obstruction.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The first and third assertions are
\Cref{thm:empirical-model-stack-interface}(ii),(iv)--(v). The wedge-of-spheres theorem
starts from a prescribed finite nerve, a constant finite abelian band, and
chosen componentwise homomorphisms
\(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A\). None of those data is selected by a bare empirical
model, so its group classification cannot be transported through the split
construction. This proves the second assertion.
\end{proof}
